docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:119:`TOUCH_BFS_RADIUS` maps to `BEAM_SOLVED_NEIGHBORHOOD_RADIUS`. CPU builds the inverse-move neighborhood around the current target. GPU searches for a neighborhood touch. The stored suffix is appended during reconstruction and the complete path is replay-validated on CPU. Experimental K2/suffix expansion remains disabled and is not exposed publicly.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:171:Field and payload sizes are bounded. Unknown fields are rejected at the public API boundary until the schema version changes.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:188:`INGEST_MODE` is a case-sensitive exact allowlist with only `normal`, `store_only`, and `reject`. Missing, empty, mixed-case, or otherwise unknown values resolve to `reject`; raw configuration values are never returned or logged. In `normal`, the HTTP producer validates the envelope, persists immutable raw R2 plus a D1 row, and sends `{submission_id}` to the Queue. In `store_only`, it validates and persists raw R2 plus a D1 row that remains `received`, but performs zero Queue sends. In `reject`, including the fail-closed missing/unknown cases, the endpoint accepts nothing and performs no R2, D1, or Queue write.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:190:In `normal`, the producer persists `received`, sends `{submission_id}` to the at-least-once Queue, and confirms the send with `received|queued|retryable -> queued`; the `queued -> queued` case is required for scheduled resend confirmation. Queue delivery may run before that confirmation, so the consumer must compare-transition `received|queued|retryable -> validating`. A successful confirmation refreshes `updated_at` and clears `ingest_paused` or any other stale `safe_error`. An ambiguous Queue failure returns `queued` only when a reread proves consumer progress beyond `queued`; otherwise an eligible `received|queued|retryable` row is confirmed `retryable`.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:192:The downstream path is `validating -> validated | rejected -> staged -> published`; exhausted work becomes `dead_letter`. Retryable failures use bounded exponential backoff. Every consumer action and state transition is idempotent. A failed enqueue attempt increments `retry_count`, refreshes `updated_at`, and records only a safe error; a later successful enqueue clears that stale error. Raw R2 storage occurs before the Worker returns an accepted receipt.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:196:The service target is at least 100 concurrent notebook clients. HTTP handlers do not call GitHub synchronously. In `normal`, they persist to R2/D1 and enqueue work, then return `202` with submission ids; in `store_only`, they persist the accepted receipt without enqueueing, as specified above. Queue consumers scale independently.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:200:A normal concurrent duplicate waits a bounded interval for the first producer and reuses its receipt without another Queue send. If the shared row is still `received` after the final reread, the duplicate resends the same `{submission_id}` and confirms `queued`/later or `retryable`; this recovery may create another Queue delivery, so downstream consumer idempotency is mandatory. All such deliveries still reference one D1 row and one retained winning raw object.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:202:The Worker scheduled handler is a strict no-op unless the resolved mode is `normal`. In `normal` it runs a bounded recovery page over stale `received|queued|retryable` rows, using the `(state,updated_at)` index and a `staleBefore` cutoff as backoff eligibility. It resends the same `{submission_id}`, uses checked state transitions, retains raw R2, and advances failed retry metadata so one hot page cannot starve older tail rows. A successful stale `queued` resend performs `queued -> queued`, refreshes `updated_at`, and clears `ingest_paused` or another stale `safe_error`, so the same row cannot flood every cron page. Thus a `store_only` row remains `received` even after it is stale; switching to `normal` lets the next scheduled run recover that same id once per eligible scan.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:204:Queue backlog delivery resolves `INGEST_MODE` before inspecting the message body. For every non-`normal` mode, including missing/unknown values, it parses only `submission_id` and conditionally parks D1 `received|queued|retryable -> retryable` with `safe_error=ingest_paused`, unchanged `retry_count`, refreshed `updated_at`, and the raw R2 reference untouched. It rereads and verifies that durable park, then ACKs; a successful park never calls `message.retry()` and therefore consumes no Cloudflare `max_retries` attempt. Before that ACK it performs no R2 read, replay, publisher enqueue, token/GitHub access, payload log, or raw-mode log. A duplicate delivery repeats the same idempotent D1 park and still leaves one row. A D1 exception or unverifiable transition is not ACKed and follows the platform retry/exhaustion path; the unchanged stale row remains recoverable. When mode returns to `normal`, scheduled recovery resends the same parked `retryable` id, and it also rescues a stale `queued` row left after park failures exhausted Queue delivery.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:219:- the exact fail-closed `normal|store_only|reject` operating-mode gate described above.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:235:The GitHub writer first persists accepted validated ids durably in Durable Object storage, then re-resolves `INGEST_MODE` immediately before any external token or GitHub API/ref/tree/commit write. If the mode is not `normal`, it makes no external request, does not mark rows `staged` or `published`, retains those durable ids, and re-arms a bounded alarm without logging payloads or the raw mode. The `setAlarm` call is part of the durable contract: if it fails, the operation throws and is retried rather than silently stranding ids. This final guard covers mode changes after Queue validation but before publication.
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:255:- exact mode tests for `normal`, `store_only`, `reject`, missing, mixed-case, and unknown values, including zero persistence in fail-closed modes;
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:256:- scheduled-mode tests proving a `store_only` row older than 60 seconds is not enqueued, stale `queued` joins `received|retryable` only in `normal`, and a successful same-id resend refreshes `updated_at` and clears `ingest_paused`/stale errors before the next bounded page;
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:260:- non-`normal` Queue backlog tests for `received|queued|retryable` proving durable `ingest_paused` parking, unchanged retry count/raw, reread verification, successful ACK with no message retry or prohibited binding access, idempotent duplicates beyond the configured `max_retries` equivalent, transition-false reread, a same-id normal resume, and D1 park exceptions through platform exhaustion followed by stale-queued recovery;
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:263:- GitHub writer final-guard tests proving ids are durable before the mode guard, non-`normal` modes make zero external requests and re-arm an alarm, `setAlarm` failure throws/retries, and `normal` resume publishes retained ids;
docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-public-cayleypy-2xt4-results-design.md:267:- recovery test proving every accepted receipt ends published, rejected with reason, or retained retryable/DLQ with its raw R2 payload.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:7:**Architecture:** In `normal`, a stateless Worker validates and persists each request to R2 and D1 before returning `202`, then enqueues per-result jobs. In `store_only` it persists accepted receipts without Queue publication, while `reject` and fail-closed modes persist nothing. Queue consumers replay solutions and update an idempotent D1 state machine. A Durable Object serializes/batches GitHub App writes to a staging branch; the results repository independently validates append-only records and deterministic indexes before auto-merge. Raw accepted payloads and exhausted failures remain recoverable in R2/DLQ.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:13:- Accept only schema version 1 and reject unknown fields.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:24:- Treat `INGEST_MODE` as the exact case-sensitive allowlist `normal|store_only|reject`; missing, empty, mixed-case, and unknown values fail closed as `reject`.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:64:- Copy and normalize: `configs/cayleypy_results_schema_v1.json`
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:99:Test a valid result and rejection for unknown field, unsupported schema, oversized author/path/proof, invalid enum, more than 100 results, and serialized request over 25 MiB. Verify errors contain JSON pointer/code only, not submitted values.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:153:- Produces: `parkPausedSubmission(db, submissionId) -> Promise<SubmissionRow>`; this reusable Task 2 primitive conditionally transitions `received|queued|retryable -> retryable` with `safe_error=ingest_paused`, leaves `retry_count` and raw R2 untouched, refreshes `updated_at`, rereads, and returns only after it verifies the durable park.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:167:    'received','queued','validating','validated','rejected','staged','published','retryable','dead_letter'
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:186:Use Miniflare bindings initialized from the exact migration bytes. Assert the deployable `state` CHECK rejects an invalid state and `PRAGMA index_info('submissions_recovery')` returns columns `state,updated_at`. Assert R2 `put` occurs before D1 received/queued state and before the returned receipt. Inject Queue failure: raw object and `retryable` row remain. Submit the same idempotency key twice: return the original submission id and enqueue once before the bounded wait expires. Also cover a stale `received` duplicate resend, crash-before-send recovery, retryable sweeping, immediate consumer progress before the producer marks `queued`, recovery duplication with one D1 row, retry-page fairness beyond `limit`, and clearing stale `safe_error` after success. Add direct real-D1 tests for parking each of `received|queued|retryable`, unchanged retry counts and raw objects, 101 idempotent duplicate parks with one row, transition-false plus verified reread, repeated D1 park exceptions, and normal stale-`queued` same-id recovery.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:217:The SQL update includes `WHERE submission_id=? AND state IN (...)`; affected row count must be one or the transition is an idempotent no-op/conflict. Successful Queue confirmation compare-transitions `received|queued|retryable -> queued`, clears `safe_error`, and refreshes `updated_at`, including `queued -> queued` after recovery resend. Failed confirmation compare-transitions `received|queued|retryable -> retryable`, increments `retry_count`, and refreshes `updated_at`; a false result is accepted only after a reread proves consumer progress beyond `queued` or a retryable state. `parkPausedSubmission` uses the same transition helper without incrementing retries and requires a reread proving `retryable` plus `ingest_paused`, whether the transition returned true or false.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:221:Query `state IN ('received','queued','retryable') AND updated_at <= ?`, ordered by `updated_at,submission_id` and capped at `limit <= 100`. For each row, resend exactly `{submission_id}`, confirm `queued`/later or `retryable` with the same checked transitions, and never remove its raw R2 object. A successful stale `queued` resend performs `queued -> queued`, refreshes `updated_at`, and clears `ingest_paused`/any stale `safe_error`, preventing cron flood and page starvation. A duplicate request that reaches its final bounded reread while still `received` uses the same resend path. Failed retries must advance `updated_at` and `retry_count` so the next bounded page can reach its tail.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:243:- Produces `type IngestMode = "normal" | "store_only" | "reject"` and one resolver that returns only those values. It accepts only exact case-sensitive matches and resolves missing, empty, mixed-case, or unknown input to `reject` without exposing the raw input.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:244:- Produces `scheduled(controller: ScheduledController, env: Env, ctx: ExecutionContext): Promise<void>` with `RECOVERY_STALE_MS = 60_000` and `RECOVERY_LIMIT = 50`. It returns before touching recovery unless the resolved mode is `normal`; otherwise it computes `staleBefore = new Date(controller.scheduledTime - RECOVERY_STALE_MS)` and calls `recoverStaleSubmissions(env, { staleBefore, limit: RECOVERY_LIMIT })`.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:248:Assert method/content-type/body-size/schema failures; per-IP `429` with `Retry-After`; accepted batch receipts; duplicate receipt; safe `404`; and health response without binding/secret detail. Cover each mode explicitly: `normal` persists R2/D1 and sends Queue work; `store_only` persists R2/D1 as `received` and sends no Queue work; exact `reject`, missing, empty, mixed-case, and arbitrary unknown values accept nothing and write nothing to R2/D1/Queue.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:257:Read `Content-Length` before body, then stream/count with a 25 MiB hard limit. Accept only `application/json`. In `normal`, persist/enqueue each result independently and return mixed receipt status without echoing result content. Keep persistence and Queue publication separate so `store_only` can leave accepted rows in `received`; reject fail-closed modes before any persistence.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:261:Use a Cloudflare Rate Limiting binding when available plus a D1/global fallback counter. Start with 30 requests/minute/IP, 100 envelopes/request, and 2,000 envelopes/minute globally; expose exact limits in `/healthz` but no infrastructure ids. Resolve the mode once through the exact allowlist: `normal` persists and queues, `store_only` persists raw R2 plus a D1 `received` row with zero Queue sends, and `reject`/missing/unknown returns a safe disabled response with zero R2/D1/Queue writes. Never echo or log the raw mode value.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:265:Add the exact `scheduled(controller, env, ctx)` interface above and configure the cron. Its normal-mode test seeds stale and fresh `received|queued|retryable` rows, proves only the bounded eligible page is resent with the same submission ids, proves successful `queued -> queued` refresh/clearing, and proves failures advance retry metadata without deleting raw R2. Additional tests prove `store_only`, `reject`, missing, and unknown modes are strict no-ops; specifically, a `store_only` row older than 60 seconds remains `received` with zero Queue sends, then switching to `normal` lets scheduled recovery enqueue that same id. Record only bounded counts, never payloads, raw mode values, or binding identifiers.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:289:- Reuses the Task 3 exact mode resolver. Every non-`normal` delivery resolves mode first, parses only `submission_id`, durably parks the D1 row through `parkPausedSubmission`, and ACKs only after verification; successful parking never calls `message.retry()` and consumes no Cloudflare `max_retries` attempt.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:293:Generate small random permutations and valid paths. Assert exact final-state equality; reject unknown move, non-bijection, wrong state length, class overflow, claimed length mismatch, altered initial/central state, and reflected path whose original-oriented candidate does not solve the original.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:297:Deliver the same message twice; deliver immediately while the producer row is still `received`; deliver from `queued` and `retryable`; deliver after state is already `validating` or `validated`; and inject malformed R2 body/hash mismatch. Assert one effective validation/publisher enqueue, terminal rejection with code, or retry/DLQ without duplicate GitHub enqueue. Duplicate and out-of-order delivery must be an idempotent no-op once another consumer owns or completed the row. For `store_only`, `reject`, missing, and unknown modes, test `received|queued|retryable` inputs and assert a successful D1 park becomes one `retryable` row with `safe_error=ingest_paused`, unchanged `retry_count`, refreshed `updated_at`, retained raw, ACK, zero `message.retry()`, and zero R2/replay/publisher/token/GitHub/payload/raw-mode-log access. Repeat delivery beyond a configured-`max_retries` equivalent, cover transition-false plus reread, switch to `normal` and prove scheduled recovery sends the same id once, and inject D1 park exceptions through Queue exhaustion before stale-`queued` recovery.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:310:Resolve mode before inspecting a message body or touching D1/R2. If it is not `normal`, parse only `submission_id`, call `parkPausedSubmission`, verify the reread, and then `message.ack()`. Do not read R2, replay, touch the publisher/token/GitHub bindings, call `message.retry()`, or log payload/raw mode. A D1 exception or unverifiable park must not ACK; it follows the platform retry/exhaustion path, leaving the row for normal stale recovery. In `normal`, compare-transition `received|queued|retryable -> validating` and clear stale `safe_error`. The `received` source is mandatory because Cloudflare Queue may deliver before the producer's post-send `queued` transition. If the transition returns false, reread: already `validating|validated|rejected|staged|published|dead_letter` is an idempotent duplicate/no-op; any other state is a checked conflict. Terminal validation errors become `rejected`; R2/D1/DO/network errors use `message.retry({delaySeconds})` with capped exponential delay. After configured attempts, write `dead_letter`, keep raw R2, and enqueue to `VALIDATE_DLQ`. Every downstream write is keyed by `submission_id`/idempotency key so recovery-created duplicate messages cannot duplicate publication.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:314:Export both `queue(batch, env)` and the Task 3 `scheduled(controller, env, ctx)` handler from the final Worker module. Add an integration test that an immediate consumer can move `received -> validating` before the producer's checked `queued` confirmation, while the scheduled handler still recovers stale `received|queued|retryable` rows. Include the D1-park-failure/exhaustion case whose unchanged stale `queued` row is resent after mode returns to `normal`.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:337:- Reuses the exact mode resolver and performs a final `normal` guard immediately before external GitHub authentication or mutation.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:345:Queue 100 unique validated ids and first assert they are committed to Durable Object storage before the final mode guard. Assert a bounded batch creates unique paths, one tree/commit/ref update, and marks D1 rows staged. Inject Git ref `422` conflict twice; writer refetches head and retries without losing/duplicating files. Existing different content at a target path is terminal corruption. For `store_only`, `reject`, missing, and unknown modes at the final guard, assert zero token/GitHub requests, zero `staged|published` transitions, durable retained validated ids, a bounded re-armed alarm, and no payload/raw-mode logs; inject `setAlarm` failure and assert the operation throws/retries without silently stranding ids, then switch to `normal` and prove the retained batch publishes.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:367:Only `safe()`-normalized identifiers from allowlisted fields affect paths. File content is canonical JSON. Batch maximum: 100 records or 5 MiB, alarm within 30 seconds.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:369:Persist validated ids durably before the final mode guard. Immediately before requesting a GitHub installation token or making any external ref/tree/commit call, re-resolve `INGEST_MODE`. Unless it is `normal`, retain those durable ids, make no external request or publication transition, and re-arm the Durable Object alarm with a bounded delay. Await `setAlarm`; if it fails, throw so the caller/runtime retries and never silently strands pending ids. This is a final defense against a mode change after validation.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:456:Assert valid returns `202` and reaches `published`; invalid returns `202` then status `rejected` with a safe validation code. Confirm raw R2 exists for both and GitHub contains only the valid record.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:480:- Produces a bounded snapshot proving every accepted receipt is `published`, terminal `rejected`, or recoverably `retryable|dead_letter` with its raw R2 object.
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:536:- [ ] **Step 3: Deploy production with `INGEST_MODE=store_only`**
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:540:- [ ] **Step 4: Enable normal mode and publish one bounded result**
docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-28-cayleypy-results-ingest.md:546:Document safe commands for health, D1 submission lookup, R2 presence, Queue/DLQ metrics, retry/replay by submission id, the exact case-sensitive `normal|store_only|reject` allowlist and fail-closed missing/unknown behavior, GitHub App key rotation, rollback to the previous Worker version, and incident evidence capture. Do not put secrets in commands.

L78: # Batching, Retries and Delays
L79: 
L80: Last updated Apr 21, 2026|Copy as Markdown|cite47†View as Markdown |cite38†Agent setup L81: 
L82: [Select]
L83: ## Batching
L84: 
L85: When configuring a cite48†consumer Worker for a queue, you can also define how messages are batched as they are delivered.
L86: 
L87: Batching can:
L88: 
L89:   1. Reduce the total number of times your consumer Worker needs to be invoked (which can reduce costs).
L90:   2. Allow you to batch messages when writing to an external API or service (reducing writes).
L91:   3. Disperse load over time, especially if your producer Workers are associated with user-facing activity.
L92: There are two ways to configure how messages are batched. You configure batching when connecting your consumer Worker to a queue.
L93: 
L94:   * `max_batch_size` - The maximum size of a batch delivered to a consumer (defaults to 10 messages).
L95:   * `max_batch_timeout` - the maximum amount of time the queue will wait before delivering a batch to a consumer (defaults to 5 seconds)
L96: 
L97: Batch size configuration
L98: Both `max_batch_size` and `max_batch_timeout` work together. Whichever limit is reached first will trigger the delivery of a batch.
L99: For example, a `max_batch_size = 30` and a `max_batch_timeout = 10` means that if 30 messages are written to the queue, the consumer will receive a batch of 30 messages. However, if it takes longer than 10 seconds for those 30 messages to be written to the queue, then the consumer will get a batch of messages that contains however many messages were on the queue at the time (somewhere between 1 and 29, in this case).
L100: 
L101: Empty queues
L102: When a queue is empty, a push-based (Worker) consumer's `queue` handler will not be invoked until there are messages to deliver. A queue does not attempt to push empty batches to a consumer and thus does not invoke unnecessary reads.
L103: 
L104: cite8†Pull-based consumers that attempt to pull from a queue, even when empty, will incur a read operation.
L105: When determining what size and timeout settings to configure, you will want to consider latency (how long can you wait to receive messages?), overall batch size (when writing to external systems), and cost (fewer-but-larger batches).
L106: ### Batch settings
L107: 
L108: The following batch-level settings can be configured to adjust how Queues delivers batches to your configured consumer.
L109: 
L110: Setting  | Default  | Minimum  | Maximum
L111: --- | --- | --- | ---
L112: Maximum Batch Size `max_batch_size`  | 10 messages  | 1 message  | 100 messages
L113: Maximum Batch Timeout `max_batch_timeout`  | 5 seconds  | 0 seconds  | 60 seconds
L114: ## Explicit acknowledgement and retries
L115: 
L116: You can acknowledge individual messages within a batch by explicitly acknowledging each message as it is processed. Messages that are explicitly acknowledged will not be re-delivered, even if your queue consumer fails on a subsequent message and/or fails to return successfully when processing a batch.
L117:   * Each message can be acknowledged as you process it within a batch, and avoids the entire batch from being re-delivered if your consumer throws an error during batch processing.
L118:   * Acknowledging individual messages is useful when you are calling external APIs, writing messages to a database, or otherwise performing non-idempotent (state changing) actions on individual messages.
L119: 
L120: To explicitly acknowledge a message as delivered, call the `ack()` method on the message.
L121: index.js js
L122: 
L123:     `export default {
L124:     	async queue(batch, env, ctx) {
L125:     		for (const msg of batch.messages) {
L126:     			// TODO: do something with the message
L127:     			// Explicitly acknowledge the message as delivered
L128:     			msg.ack();
L129:     		}
L130:     	},
L131:     };`
L132: index.ts ts
L133: 
L134:     `export default {
L135:     	async queue(batch, env, ctx): Promise<void> {
L136:     		for (const msg of batch.messages) {
L137:     			// TODO: do something with the message
L138:     			// Explicitly acknowledge the message as delivered
L139:     			msg.ack();
L140:     		}
L141:     	},
L142:     } satisfies ExportedHandler<Env>;`
L143: 
L144:     `from workers import WorkerEntrypoint
L145: 
L146:     class Default(WorkerEntrypoint):
L147:         async def queue(self, batch):
L148:             for msg in batch.messages:
L149:                 # TODO: do something with the message
L150:                 # Explicitly acknowledge the message as delivered
L151:                 msg.ack()`
L152: You can also call `retry()` to explicitly force a message to be redelivered in a subsequent batch. This is referred to as "negative acknowledgement". This can be particularly useful when you want to process the rest of the messages in that batch without throwing an error that would force the entire batch to be redelivered.
L153: index.js js
L154: 
L155:     `export default {
L156:     	async queue(batch, env, ctx) {
L157:     		for (const msg of batch.messages) {
L158:     			// TODO: do something with the message that fails
L159:     			msg.retry();
L160:     		}
L161:     	},
L162:     };`
L163: index.ts ts
L164: 
L165:     `export default {
L166:     	async queue(batch, env, ctx): Promise<void> {
L167:     		for (const msg of batch.messages) {
L168:     			// TODO: do something with the message that fails
L169:     			msg.retry();
L170:     		}
L171:     	},
L172:     } satisfies ExportedHandler<Env>;`
L173: 
L174:     `from workers import WorkerEntrypoint
L175: 
L176:     class Default(WorkerEntrypoint):
L177:         async def queue(self, batch):
L178:             for msg in batch.messages:
L179:                 # TODO: do something with the message that fails
L180:                 msg.retry()`
L181: You can also acknowledge or negatively acknowledge messages at a batch level with `ackAll()` and `retryAll()`. Calling `ackAll()` on the batch of messages (`MessageBatch`) delivered to your consumer Worker has the same behaviour as a consumer Worker that successfully returns (does not throw an error).
L182: 
L183: Note that calls to `ack()`, `retry()` and their `ackAll()` / `retryAll()` equivalents follow the below precedence rules:
L184:   * If you call `ack()` on a message, subsequent calls to `ack()` or `retry()` are silently ignored.
L185:   * If you call `retry()` on a message and then call `ack()`: the `ack()` is ignored. The first method call wins in all cases.
L186:   * If you call either `ack()` or `retry()` on a single message, and then either/any of `ackAll()` or `retryAll()` on the batch, the call on the single message takes precedence. That is, the batch-level call does not apply to that message (or messages, if multiple calls were made).
L187: ## Delivery failure
L188: 
L189: When a message is failed to be delivered, the default behaviour is to retry delivery three times before marking the delivery as failed. You can set `max_retries` (defaults to 3) when configuring your consumer, but in most cases we recommend leaving this as the default.
L190: 
L191: Messages that reach the configured maximum retries will be deleted from the queue, or if a cite7†dead-letter queue (DLQ) is configured, written to the DLQ instead.
L192: 
L193: Note
L194: Each retry counts as an additional read operation per cite27†Queues pricing .
L195: 
L196: When a single message within a batch fails to be delivered, the entire batch is retried, unless you have cite49†explicitly acknowledged a message (or messages) within that batch. For example, if a batch of 10 messages is delivered, but the 8th message fails to be delivered, all 10 messages will be retried and thus redelivered to your consumer in full.
L197: 
L198: Retried messages and consumer concurrency
L199: Retrying messages with `retry()` or calling `retryAll()` on a batch will not cause the consumer to autoscale down if consumer concurrency is enabled. Refer to cite9†Consumer concurrency to learn more.
L200: ## Delay messages
L201: 
L202: When publishing messages to a queue, or when cite49†marking a message or batch for retry , you can choose to delay messages from being processed for a period of time.
L203: 
L204: Delaying messages allows you to defer tasks until later, and/or respond to backpressure when consuming from a queue. For example, if an upstream API you are calling to returns a `HTTP 429: Too Many Requests`, you can delay messages to slow down how quickly you are consuming them before they are re-processed.
L205: Messages can be delayed by up to 24 hours.
L206: 
L207: Note
L208: 
L209: Configuring delivery and retry delays via the `wrangler` CLI or when cite11†developing locally requires `wrangler` version `3.38.0` or greater. Use `npx wrangler@latest` to always use the latest version of `wrangler`.
L210: ### Delay on send
L211: 
L212: To delay a message or batch of messages when sending to a queue, you can provide a `delaySeconds` parameter when sending a message.
L213: index.js js
L214: 
L215:     `// Delay a singular message by 600 seconds (10 minutes)
L216:     await env.YOUR_QUEUE.send(message, { delaySeconds: 600 });
L217: 
L218:     // Delay a batch of messages by 300 seconds (5 minutes)
L219:     await env.YOUR_QUEUE.sendBatch(messages, { delaySeconds: 300 });
L220: 
L221:     // Do not delay this message.
L222:     // If there is a global delay configured on the queue, ignore it.
L223:     await env.YOUR_QUEUE.sendBatch(messages, { delaySeconds: 0 });`
L224: index.ts ts
L225: 
L226:     `// Delay a singular message by 600 seconds (10 minutes)
L227:     await env.YOUR_QUEUE.send(message, { delaySeconds: 600 });
L228: 
L229:     // Delay a batch of messages by 300 seconds (5 minutes)
L230:     await env.YOUR_QUEUE.sendBatch(messages, { delaySeconds: 300 });
L231: 
L232:     // Do not delay this message.
L233:     // If there is a global delay configured on the queue, ignore it.
L234:     await env.YOUR_QUEUE.sendBatch(messages, { delaySeconds: 0 });`
L235:     `# Delay a singular message by 600 seconds (10 minutes)
L236:     await env.YOUR_QUEUE.send(message, delaySeconds=600)
L237: 
L238:     # Delay a batch of messages by 300 seconds (5 minutes)
L239:     await env.YOUR_QUEUE.sendBatch(messages, delaySeconds=300)
L240: 
L241:     # Do not delay this message.
L242:     # If there is a global delay configured on the queue, ignore it.
L243:     await env.YOUR_QUEUE.sendBatch(messages, delaySeconds=0)`
L244: You can also configure a default, global delay on a per-queue basis by passing `--delivery-delay-secs` when creating a queue via the `wrangler` CLI:
L245: 
L246:     `# Delay all messages by 5 minutes as a default
L247:     npx wrangler queues create $QUEUE-NAME --delivery-delay-secs=300`
L248: ### Delay on retry
L249: 
L250: When cite50†consuming messages from a queue , you can choose to cite49†explicitly mark messages to be retried . Messages can be retried and delayed individually, or as an entire batch.
L251: 
L252: To delay an individual message within a batch:
L253: index.js js
L254: 
L255:     `export default {
L256:     	async queue(batch, env, ctx) {
L257:     		for (const msg of batch.messages) {
L258:     			// Mark for retry and delay a singular message
L259:     			// by 3600 seconds (1 hour)
L260:     			msg.retry({ delaySeconds: 3600 });
L261:     		}
L262:     	},
L263:     };`
L264: index.ts ts
L265: 
L266:     `export default {
L267:     	async queue(batch, env, ctx): Promise<void> {
L268:     		for (const msg of batch.messages) {
L269:     			// Mark for retry and delay a singular message
L270:     			// by 3600 seconds (1 hour)
L271:     			msg.retry({ delaySeconds: 3600 });
L272:     		}
L273:     	},
L274:     } satisfies ExportedHandler<Env>;`
L275: 
L276:     `from workers import WorkerEntrypoint
L277: 
L278:     class Default(WorkerEntrypoint):
L279:         async def queue(self, batch):
L280:             for msg in batch.messages:
L281:                 # Mark for retry and delay a singular message
L282:                 # by 3600 seconds (1 hour)
L283:                 msg.retry(delaySeconds=3600)`
L284: To delay a batch of messages:
L285: 
L286: index.js js
L287: 
L288:     `export default {
L289:     	async queue(batch, env, ctx) {
L290:     		// Mark for retry and delay a batch of messages
L291:     		// by 600 seconds (10 minutes)
L292:     		batch.retryAll({ delaySeconds: 600 });
L293:     	},
L294:     };`
L295: index.ts ts
L296: 
L297:     `export default {
L298:     	async queue(batch, env, ctx): Promise<void> {
L299:     		// Mark for retry and delay a batch of messages
L300:     		// by 600 seconds (10 minutes)
L301:     		batch.retryAll({ delaySeconds: 600 });
L302:     	},
L303:     } satisfies ExportedHandler<Env>;`
L304: 
L305:     `from workers import WorkerEntrypoint
L306: 
L307:     class Default(WorkerEntrypoint):
L308:         async def queue(self, batch):
L309:             # Mark for retry and delay a batch of messages
L310:             # by 600 seconds (10 minutes)
L311:             batch.retryAll(delaySeconds=600)`
L312: You can also choose to set a default retry delay to any messages that are retried due to either implicit failure or when calling `retry()` explicitly. This is set at the consumer level, and is supported in both push-based (Worker) and pull-based (HTTP) consumers.
L313: Delays can be configured via the `wrangler` CLI:
L314: 
L315:     `# Push-based consumers
L316:     # Delay any messages that are retried by 60 seconds (1 minute) by default.
L317:     npx wrangler@latest queues consumer worker add $QUEUE-NAME $WORKER_SCRIPT_NAME --retry-delay-secs=60
L318: 
L319:     # Pull-based consumers
L320:     # Delay any messages that are retried by 60 seconds (1 minute) by default.
L321:     npx wrangler@latest queues consumer http add $QUEUE-NAME --retry-delay-secs=60`
L322: Delays can also be configured in the cite51†Wrangler configuration file with the `delivery_delay` setting for producers (when sending) and/or the `retry_delay` (when retrying) per-consumer:
L323: 
L324:     `{
L325:     	"queues": {
L326:     		"producers": [
L327:     			{
L328:     				"binding": "<BINDING_NAME>",
L329:     				"queue": "<QUEUE-NAME>",
L330:     				"delivery_delay": 60 // delay every message delivery by 1 minute
L331:     			}
L332:     		],
L333:     		"consumers": [
L334:     			{
L335:     				"queue": "my-queue",
L336:     				"retry_delay": 300 // delay any retried message by 5 minutes before re-attempting delivery
L337:     			}
L338:     		]
L339:     	}
L340:     }`
L341: 
L342:     `[[queues.producers]]
L343:     binding = "<BINDING_NAME>"
L344:     queue = "<QUEUE-NAME>"
L345:     delivery_delay = 60
L346: 
L347:     [[queues.consumers]]
L348:     queue = "my-queue"
L349:     retry_delay = 300`
L350: If you use both the `wrangler` CLI and the cite52†Wrangler configuration file to change the settings associated with a queue or a queue consumer, the most recent configuration change will take effect.
L351: 
L352: Refer to the cite53†Queues REST API documentation to learn how to configure message delays and retry delays programmatically.
L353: ### Message delay precedence
L354: 
L355: Messages can be delayed by default at the queue level, or per-message (or batch).
L356:   * Per-message/batch delay settings take precedence over queue-level settings.
L357:   * Setting `delaySeconds: 0` on a message when sending or retrying will ignore any queue-level delays and cause the message to be delivered in the next batch.
L358:   * A message sent or retried with `delaySeconds: <any positive integer>` to a queue with a shorter default delay will still respect the message-level setting.
L359: ### Apply a backoff algorithm
L360: 
L361: You can apply a backoff algorithm to increasingly delay messages based on the current number of attempts to deliver the message.
L362: 
L363: Each message delivered to a consumer includes an `attempts` property that tracks the number of delivery attempts made.
L364: 
L365: For example, to generate an cite54†exponential backoff ↗†en.wikipedia.org for a message, you can create a helper function that calculates this for you:
L366: index.js js
L367: 
L368:     `function calculateExponentialBackoff(attempts, baseDelaySeconds) {
L369:     	return baseDelaySeconds ** attempts;
L370:     }`
L371: 
L372: index.ts ts
L373: 
L374:     `function calculateExponentialBackoff(
L375:     	attempts: number,
L376:     	baseDelaySeconds: number,
L377:     ): number {
L378:     	return baseDelaySeconds ** attempts;
L379:     }`
L380: 
L381:     `def calculate_exponential_backoff(attempts, base_delay_seconds):
L382:         return base_delay_seconds ** attempts`
L383: In your consumer, you then pass the value of `msg.attempts` and your desired delay factor as the argument to `delaySeconds` when calling `retry()` on an individual message:
L384: index.js js
L385: 
L386:     `const BASE_DELAY_SECONDS = 30;
L387: 
L388:     export default {
L389:     	async queue(batch, env, ctx) {
L390:     		for (const msg of batch.messages) {
L391:     			// Mark for retry with exponential backoff
L392:     			msg.retry({
L393:     				delaySeconds: calculateExponentialBackoff(
L394:     					msg.attempts,
L395:     					BASE_DELAY_SECONDS,
L396:     				),
L397:     			});
L398:     		}
L399:     	},
L400:     };`
L401: index.ts ts
L402: 
L403:     `const BASE_DELAY_SECONDS = 30;
L404: 
L405:     export default {
L406:     	async queue(batch, env, ctx): Promise<void> {
L407:     		for (const msg of batch.messages) {
L408:     			// Mark for retry with exponential backoff
L409:     			msg.retry({
L410:     				delaySeconds: calculateExponentialBackoff(
L411:     					msg.attempts,
L412:     					BASE_DELAY_SECONDS,
L413:     				),
L414:     			});
L415:     		}
L416:     	},
L417:     } satisfies ExportedHandler<Env>;`
L418: 
L419:     `from workers import WorkerEntrypoint
L420: 
L421:     BASE_DELAY_SECONDS = 30
L422:     class Default(WorkerEntrypoint):
L423:         async def queue(self, batch):
L424:             for msg in batch.messages:
L425:                 # Mark for retry and delay a singular message
L426:                 # by 3600 seconds (1 hour)
L427:                 msg.retry(
L428:                     delaySeconds=calculate_exponential_backoff(
L429:                         msg.attempts,
L430:                         BASE_DELAY_SECONDS,
L431:                     )
L432:                 )`

# Work With Kaggle

Use this skill whenever the task involves Kaggle notebooks, kernels, datasets, or GPU validation runs.

## Project Memory First

For project repositories, read local project guidance before changing notebooks or source:

- `AGENTS.md`
- `memory/PROMPTS.md` and `memory/CHANGELOG.md`, especially recent Kaggle entries
- relevant `test_results/kaggle_*` reports for the same failure/config family

This project has many Kaggle-specific decisions preserved in memory. Do not assume a generic Kaggle workflow is correct when project memory says otherwise.

## Safety And Scope

- Do not upload or publish current uncommitted source to a Kaggle dataset unless the user explicitly approves external export.
- Prefer GitHub-backed Kaggle source when the user has selected GitHub as source of truth.
- Do not push a public Kaggle notebook/kernel unless the user explicitly approves publication or public push.
- Do not make C++/CUDA algorithmic changes while diagnosing Kaggle failures unless the user explicitly approves code changes. Config-only notebook changes are acceptable when useful and consistent with the request.
- Do not revert unrelated local changes. Kaggle notebook edits often coexist with dirty source changes.
- For multi-rank validation, do not fake `WORLD_SIZE=2` on one GPU with both ranks on `LOCAL_RANK=0` unless the user explicitly approves that limited smoke. Real multi-rank validation should use real multi-GPU hardware such as Kaggle 2xT4.

## Kaggle CLI Workflow

Prefer Kaggle CLI for authenticated Kaggle work:

- Check metadata before pushing: `kaggle/kernel-metadata.json` or the selected push package metadata.
- Validate notebooks locally before push: JSON parse and Python AST parse for code cells.
- Push with the intended package directory, then monitor with `kaggle kernels status <owner/slug>`.
- Download outputs/logs after completion or failure and store them under `test_results/`.
- If CLI network/proxy behavior is project-specific, inspect existing scripts/config and preserve local proxy-bypass behavior instead of switching tools silently.

When reporting command output to the user, summarize the important status/version/error lines. The user does not see raw command output.

## Notebook Preparation Rules

For solver notebooks in this project:

- Keep a first config cell with the main run parameters such as beam, depth, puzzle range, model weights URL/path, history mode, and torchrun topology.
- Keep build/checkouts and large transient trees under `/tmp` where possible. Use `/kaggle/working` for logs, CSVs, summaries, and final artifacts.
- Add a preflight cell for large runs: compute local/global beam, world size, shard capacity, Stream1/3/4 guards, static history RAM/disk budgets, and free disk under `/tmp` before clone/build/run.
- Clean stale `/tmp/beam_history_*` and configured history disk paths before runs and between puzzles when static-hybrid history is used.
- For long multi-rank runs, stream selected rank logs live while writing full rank logs to disk.
- Final cells should summarize results and produce user-useful CSV/PNG artifacts, for example solved counts and solution-length histograms.

## Torchrun And Multi-GPU Rules

- Torchrun notebooks should launch the existing binary/processes through explicit topology settings, not implicit GPU auto-detection.
- Expose `TORCHRUN_NPROC_PER_NODE`, `TORCHRUN_NNODES`, `TORCHRUN_NODE_RANK`, and rendezvous endpoint/port in config.
- Derive `WORLD_SIZE` from torchrun topology settings, not from `torch.cuda.device_count()` alone.
- For C++ multi-rank runs, torchrun or Python launchers should only start processes. C++ reads `RANK`, `LOCAL_RANK`, and `WORLD_SIZE`, calls `cudaSetDevice(LOCAL_RANK)`, and creates NCCL communicators itself.
- H100 and T4 notebooks may differ by CUDA architecture/build directory and hardware-specific config, but should otherwise stay comparable when the user asks for paired notebooks.

## Monitoring And Stop Rules

- For diagnostic Kaggle runs, if the user has asked Codex to watch logs and no new logs appear for about 120 seconds, stop the run and inspect downloaded logs. This rule came from project memory and applies to diagnostic/watch tasks.
- Do not stop production/no-timeout runs unless the user explicitly asks or the notebook itself has a configured timeout.
- If a run appears hung because stdout is redirected, check rank log files and whether live-rank streaming is enabled before assuming a stall.
- Preserve logs even when stopping: terminate gracefully when possible, then download output files.

## Failure Diagnosis Checklist

After a Kaggle failure, collect and scan:

- Kaggle worker status and kernel version
- notebook stdout tail
- rank logs under the notebook output directory
- result JSONL/CSV summaries
- GPU budget/preflight output
- explicit Kaggle messages such as disk quota failures

Common patterns in this project:

- `KernelWorkerStatus.ERROR` plus "more disk space than is available" usually means disk quota/static history arena pressure.
- `return_code=-9` can indicate host RAM OOM.
- `code=3002` / `STREAM_FATAL_STREAM3_DOUBLE_BUFFER_OVERFLOW` points to Stream3/Stream4/threshold/capacity flow diagnostics.
- `final selected count does not match score phase counts` points to final selection/histogram/exact-count mismatch.
- `cudaGraphInstantiate: out of memory` may require config-only headroom/shard-capacity changes before deeper code work.
- Silent output after build may simply mean rank stdout is redirected and live rank logging is disabled.

Write a short report under `test_results/` for meaningful Kaggle validations, sweeps, failures, or launches.

## Response Style

- Be explicit about whether you changed source code, notebook config only, or only launched/monitored a run.
- Include Kaggle kernel slug/version/status when known.
- Include exact local report/log paths in `test_results/`.
- Keep recommendations tied to evidence from logs, not guesses.
- If the next step requires exporting source or publishing publicly, ask for explicit approval first.
